## Snapshot
GPU "neocloud" — rents NVIDIA accelerated compute (GPU-as-a-service) to AI labs, hyperscalers and enterprises on multi-year contracts. IPO'd March 2025; short public history. FY2025 revenue **$5.1B, +168% YoY** (10-K FY2025, 2026-03-02), essentially all from AI cloud compute. Differentiated by purpose-built AI datacenters, early NVIDIA allocation (Exemplar Cloud / GB200 first-mover) and scale of contracted power. The model is capital-intensive and debt-funded: it converts a massive contracted backlog into revenue by building/leasing datacenters and buying GPUs ahead of the revenue ramp. Sits squarely in the AI infrastructure chain between NVIDIA silicon and the frontier-model demand layer.

## At a glance — product · buyer · supplier
| | |
|---|---|
| **Sells (top 3)** | 1) Multi-year reserved GPU cloud compute (GPU-as-a-service) · 2) Leading-edge GB200/Blackwell NVL72 clusters (Exemplar Cloud) · 3) Inference compute (>50% of usage) |
| **Main buyer(s)** | Hyperscalers & AI labs — Microsoft (~67% of FY25 rev), OpenAI (~$11.9B+$4B), Meta, Google; top-2 ~65% of Q1-26 rev |
| **Key suppliers** | NVIDIA GPUs (GB200/Blackwell); datacenter/power lessors (3.5+ GW contracted); debt capital (~$25B DDTL facilities) |

## Position in the value chain
CoreWeave is the financing-and-operating layer that turns GPUs + power + debt into rentable AI compute. Its edge is a $99.4B backlog (Q1-26) and NVIDIA-preferred allocation; its fragility is that the same backlog is funded with ~$25B of debt and $8.2B+ of operating-lease liabilities, against revenue still ~65% concentrated in its top two customers. Strong demand visibility, thin equity cushion, high customer concentration.

Price used $85.43 (21-Sep-2026); market cap $47.1bn; 551m shares; free float 69%. Every estimate below is UBS's unless marked otherwise.)_

- 🔴🔴🔴 **THE CALL AND THE VALUATION BRIDGE, IN FULL.** **Buy, 12m PT $120 = 3.8x CY28E sales of $45,575m**, deliberately set **below the peer-group average/median of ~4.5x**: *"We believe CoreWeave should be valued closer to the peer group median of 4.5x due to its upper-quartile revenue growth, improving revenues per GW, and strong checks on performance/reliability. We are **not yet** valuing CoreWeave with an above-peer multiple given its **higher leverage profile, interest-expense burden, high customer concentration, and still early progress in moving up the stack.**"* Forecast price appreciation **+40.5%**, forecast excess return **+30.7%** against a 9.8% market-return assumption. Scenario spread: **UPSIDE $190** (4.8x CY28E revs $46,995m; 66% growth, 30% GM, 21.8% OM) · **BASE $120** (3.8x on $45,575m; 61% growth, 28% GM, 19.9% OM) · **DOWNSIDE $46** (2.7x on $44,155m; 56% growth, 26% GM, 18% OM). ⚠️ **Note the construction: the entire $46→$190 range is driven by the MULTIPLE and by margin, not by revenue — UBS's own downside case still has CY28 revenue at $44.2bn, i.e. only 3% below base. - 🔴 **LEASES, QUANTIFIED — AND ONE SITE IS 41% OF THE NOT-YET-COMMENCED BOOK.** Total lease obligations (operating + finance, **average term 12 years**) **$16.5bn at end-2Q26, doubled over two quarters**; plus **$35.5bn of "not yet commenced" lease payments, *"of which $14.7 billion or 41% is associated with a SINGLE data center site"*** — **$52bn all in**. UBS treats the not-yet-commenced line as *"a directional indicator of future capex (clearly up)."*
- ⚠️🔴 **THE UP-THE-STACK LEG IS THE WEAKEST PART OF THE BULL CASE AND UBS SAYS SO.** **95% of revenue is still core GPU compute.** Margin-accretive businesses (storage, CPU, networking, software) **>$400m ARR at end-2Q26 = 4% of mix**; *"software" revenue on track for **$100m+ at end-2026***; *"Frankly, this exposure remains very small for CoreWeave despite the $1 billion Weights & Biases deal in May 2025."* **Managed Inference launched Mar/Apr 2026, $100m ARR at 2Q26, expected to reach $250m exiting 2026 (1-2% of total company ARR)** — ⚠️ **the $250m exit is the net-new FORWARD number; management's own 09-21 framing on this page was the growth ("from one to more than 100" ARR), not the exit target.** UBS's own view on where to go: not databases or apps, but *"more compute-adjacent services such as **cyber-security and infrastructure monitoring**"*, quoting Ilya Sutskever — *"neo-clouds have limited cybersecurity… next time agents successfully go rogue, they'll try taking over a neo-cloud to run more copies"* — which corroborates the SemiAnalysis ClusterMAX thread already on this page from 09-02.
- 🔴🔴 **THE EVIDENCE-LAB SURVEY IS THE HARDEST NEGATIVE DATAPOINT IN THE NOTE, AND IT GOES STRAIGHT AT THE ENTERPRISE-BROADENING LEG.** UBS's 6th semi-annual **US AI Business Survey (~140 organisations)** on preferred AI-infrastructure provider: **Microsoft maintained (in fact slightly increased) a material lead, AWS #2, Google Cloud #3 — and *"the neo-clouds (CoreWeave, Lambda) were referenced by <10% of respondents and didn't show any material improvement from prior surveys."*** UBS's own gloss: *"this is the opportunity for CoreWeave and clear signs of success would be impactful to investor sentiment."* ➤ **Set this against the 08-11 release's enterprise name-drops and the 09-14 "75% of customers use at least three services" attach rate: the attach rate is about DEPTH within a narrow base; the survey is about BREADTH, and breadth is not moving.**
- 🔴🔴 **THE CUSTOMER CHECKS — THREE OF THEM, PRINTED RAW, AND ONE IS A CHURN EVENT.** UBS says it would *"rather ground our views about CoreWeave's competitive differentiation in what customers… say, not in what CoreWeave management says."*
  - ✅ **Customer 1 (mid-8-figure ~$50m annual AI compute budget, ~1/3 on neoclouds today, expects ~50% in 12-18 months):** *"we used to only use the hyperscalers but they have proven to be too slow and too expensive, the neo-clouds can stand up capacity much faster and they're also **30-45% less expensive on the same Nvidia GPUs**."* On CRWV specifically: *"They have the **highest price points for training runs, maybe 10-15% higher than peers**, but they've offered **50-60% discounts off list**, as we signed a 3-year commitment… **When you have a large training run, no one beats CoreWeave, this is their invisible advantage, reliability on high-volume jobs. They have the least amount of idle GPUs and the deepest software stack.**"* ⚠️ **And the offsets from the same customer: CRWV's Managed Inference is *"playing catch-up to the Token Factory service of [[NBIS]]"*, CRWV and NBIS are *"about 1-2 quarters behind the pure-play inference players"*, *"you have to buy a minimum 8-GPU rack from CoreWeave, while others like Nebius will allow you to literally buy access to a single chip"*, and *"Nebius covers Europe better than CoreWeave and in certain European regions, you can't even work with CoreWeave."***
  - ⚠️ **Customer 2** uses CRWV for training and fine-tuning and moves inference to hyperscalers for data management/security/access control; on the security thread: *"**log monitoring is weak or missing on CoreWeave**, but remember, we use them mostly for pre-production workloads."*
  - 🔴🔴 **Customer 3 — the churn: *"we quite recently ENDED the CoreWeave relationship and shifted those workloads to our own data centers. We are one of the largest banks in the world and have made a big commitment to scale our on-premise infrastructure, as we want to have control over our AI stack."*** ➤ **A named-category (global bank) enterprise logo leaving, printed inside a Buy initiation. That is the first customer-loss datapoint of any kind on this page, and it lands in exactly the enterprise vertical — financials, alongside Jane Street — that the diversification story leans on.**
  - **And the renewal-pricing fork, two customers, opposite answers:** *"we have a fixed-price 3-year contract with CoreWeave that will run off soon and while we do plan to renew, we are expecting a **30% uplift** in GPU per hour pricing given the supply shortage. There is no room to negotiate, it's a seller's market… **CoreWeave is essentially marking-to-market as contracts roll off**"* versus *"**Anything close to a 20-30% price hike is out of the picture; if they tried that we would evaluate alternative vendors.**"* ➤ **This is the re-contracting ASP debate (DB's 75%-vs-50% of original ASP, § Unit economics) answered by two customers who disagree — and it is now an UPSIDE argument on both sides of 100%, not a haircut debate.**
- 🔴 **COMPETITION — 17 NAMED PARTICIPANTS, AND THE THREE ENTRANTS UBS SAYS HAVE WEIGHED ON THE STOCK ARE SPCX, META AND SB ENERGY.** New/adjacent supply it sizes: **SpaceX** at 1.4 GW of capacity (+400 MW in the Jun quarter) to 2 GW by end-2026 and *"up to 10 GW by the end of 2027 (an aggressive target, with SpaceX signaling that ~7 GW would be a reasonable estimate to model)"*, with **$26bn of annualised revenue from just the Anthropic and Google deals — *"substantially larger than CoreWeave"* at run-rate**; ⚠️ **and the direct hit: *"Cursor, now acquired by SpaceX, is a CoreWeave customer, and hence we need to monitor the potential that Cursor may wind down its CoreWeave exposure and consolidate on SpaceX's infrastructure."*** **[[META]]** confirmed on its recent call it is exploring *"potentially selling compute directly"* — the risk being not just a rival but a large CRWV customer with less reason to expand. **SB Energy** (SoftBank) is building OpenAI's up-to-8 GW Pike County, Ohio site (first 800 MW targeted 2028; first 4.25 GW for OpenAI) under a **20-year lease**, with NVDA investing $1.5bn and providing credit support — *"OpenAI is **not** turning to its incumbent GPU data center operators — Microsoft, Oracle and CoreWeave — for this build."* Nscale, Lambda ($35bn Anthropic deal reported, unconfirmed), Crusoe (6 GW contracted, 1 GW delivered) and the inference layer (Fireworks *"now at $1+ billion in revenues, up from $400 million in January"*, Baseten, Together) round it out. ⚠️ **On the inference platforms UBS makes the point that they are CUSTOMERS of the neoclouds, not only rivals — one told a UBS conference it started on the big four and *"now we run on top of more than twenty, including many neo-clouds."***
- ✅ **WHAT UBS DELIBERATELY DOES NOT ARGUE, because it matters for how the call can be falsified:** *"It may surprise some readers that we're actually going to devote relatively little space in this report to the near-term demand trends… **Few investors are debating the strength of demand for AI compute**, hence we did not focus heavily on the demand backdrop."* Its stated position on the pacing scare is that it fades: at the Santa Clara AI Infrastructure conference *"(we were in the audience), OpenAI's compute head said that a greater focus on safety and alignment would likely **INCREASE** OpenAI's demand for compute."* ➤ **So the Buy is, on UBS's own framing, a call on SENTIMENT ROTATION plus REVENUE-PER-GW — *"a positive call on CoreWeave shares is a de facto call that investors will soon rotate back into the long-AI trade."* If demand is not the crux and the multiple is, the falsification test is the 4Q26 14% implied OM and the revenue-per-GW print, not the backlog.**
- **Housekeeping, logged so it is not read as a disclosure:** UBS's company description says the platform *"spans more than 40 AI-focused data centers"* — management said *"more than 50 data centers now active"* on 09-21. UBS also flags a forward event already on this page: *"We'll look for incremental context at CoreWeave's upcoming customer event in SF on **Sept 30/Oct 1**."* And the Quantitative Research Review page for CRWV is answered **"No Catalyst"** with every question N/A.

**🆕🔴🔴🔴 2026-09-21 — THE FIRST COREWEAVE PRIMARY SOURCE IN THIS ARCHIVE, AND IT PUTS A THREE-RUNG PRICING LADDER, CONTRACT-LEVEL EBITDA MARGINS AND A PAYBACK RANGE ON THE RECORD — PLUS THE BASIS CONTEXT THAT LARGELY SETTLES THE ~$40M/MW UNIT HAZARD THIS PAGE FLAGGED FOUR DAYS AGO.**

_(Source: **Nick Robbins, Vice President of Corporate Development, CoreWeave** — **BTG Pactual Global Access** client websession, Zoom, questions read from a written queue, **2026-09-21** 17:00-17:30 BRT · **Capstone notes (Notion AI transcript)**, archived [`CRWV/transcripts/CRWV_IR-hosted-investor-QA_2026-09-21.md`](../CRWV/transcripts/CRWV_IR-hosted-investor-QA_2026-09-21.md). Name and title CONFIRMED from the Outlook invite, not from the transcript. ⚠️⚠️ **MANAGEMENT SOURCE HOSTED BY A BROKER'S ACCESS DESK — BTG Pactual is the venue, not the author: no rating, no price target, no estimate, no model appears anywhere in it.** ⚠️⚠️ **MACHINE TRANSCRIPTION OF AN UNLABELLED MULTI-SPEAKER ROOM: speaker turns are not marked, questions and answers run together, and proper nouns garble ("Corweave"/"Corpus" = CoreWeave, "catcall" = cap-call, "co-ops" ≈ CoWoS *(decode UNCONFIRMED)*). Quotes below are verbatim from the transcript; nothing here supersedes a filing, and every surprising figure should be re-checked against the 10-Q.)_

- 🔴🔴🔴 **THE PRICING LADDER, WHICH IS THE REASON THIS SOURCE MATTERS — THREE RUNGS, ALL FROM ONE ANSWER.** The question cites a recent Anthropic deal *"closed three weeks ago… reached $16 per watt per year"* and asks whether it is a reasonable benchmark. Robbins:
  1. **THE FLOOR:** *"In the current environment, that 15, 16, honestly, it probably seems a little bit low… I would say that that mid-teens is probably the **absolute floor**. Maybe it's even a bit low versus being the ceiling."*
  2. **LONG-DATED (4-6 yr), THE FOUNDATION OF THE BOOK:** *"long dated contracts today are probably for **Vera Rubin clearing higher than that mid-teens figure**"* — the contracts *"[that have] allowed us to scale efficiently and finance efficiently."*
  3. **SHORT-DATED, INCLUDING PRIOR GENERATIONS:** *"shorter dated contracts, even for prior generations like… Blackwell or Grace Blackwell are pricing **meaningfully above**"* — elsewhere *"way, way higher"* — and *"[we] announced that we had signed in **Q3 short dated contracts at about 40 million a megawatt**."*
- 🔴🔴 **➤ WHAT THIS DOES TO THE 09-17 UNIT HAZARD ON THIS PAGE. The Barclays block below carries an explicit instruction not to net ~$40mn/MW against the ~$16mn/MW/YEAR base because *"the note does NOT state whether $40mn/MW is TOTAL CONTRACT VALUE or ANNUALISED."* Robbins reaches the same ~$40M/MW figure inside an answer whose stated benchmark unit is `$ per watt per YEAR`, presenting it as the top rung of one continuous ladder — which makes the ANNUALISED reading far the more natural one. ✅ And on that reading it lands exactly on the tier structure already canonical in [`_meta/assumptions.md`](_meta/assumptions.md) (`rev-per-gw`: wholesale hyperscaler/lab 5-6 yr take-or-pay ~$12-16bn per IT-GW/yr · merchant 1-3 yr term ~$20-25bn · 3-6 month spot ~$40-50bn), i.e. ~2.5x the wholesale tier rather than a 2.5x price increase on the same contract shape.** ⚠️⚠️ **HE STILL NEVER STATES THE TERM OR THE BASIS IN WORDS. Logged as STRONGLY IMPLIED ANNUAL, NOT CONFIRMED — the four-basis guard in the 09-17 block stands unchanged, and nothing here may be chained into a capex series.**
- ⚠️ **COUNTERPARTY ID FLAG ON THE BENCHMARK, BECAUSE THE QUESTION IS GARBLED: the questioner says *"Ben's KOD with Anthropic"* (machine transcript). **CoreWeave's own Anthropic deal is only ~$2-3bn / 70MW (Apr-26)** and does not produce $16/W/yr on any term. This wiki's own arithmetic on the **Nscale S-1 (2026-09-18)** puts the **Nscale–Anthropic Monarch tranche at ~$16.2bn per IT-GW per year** — the same number, and announced ~3.5 weeks before this call. **PROBABLE but NOT CONFIRMED: Robbins never names the counterparty or the seller, and neither does the questioner intelligibly.**
- 🔭🔴 **VARIANT PERCEPTION — THIS SOURCE SITS ON THE OPPOSITE SIDE OF A NUMBER THIS WIKI SETTLED THREE DAYS EARLIER.** `assumptions.md` concluded off the filed Nscale book (09-18) that the 2H26 shortage has **NOT** pushed realised wholesale rev/GW above ~$12-16bn, *"even the new Vera Rubin lab tranche only reaches ~$16.2bn"*, and that the spread is **TIER, not chip**. Robbins accepts the tier structure and then **puts the FLOOR at the top of that wholesale band and says CoreWeave's own long-dated Vera Rubin book clears above it.** ➤ **Two readings, three days apart, on the same quantity: a filed S-1 from a smaller operator says mid-teens is the ceiling for lab-credit take-or-pay; the largest independent operator says mid-teens is the floor. NEITHER ADOPTED. If both are true the difference is operator quality — which is precisely the ~2x "a top-operator GW ≈ 2x a generic GW" spread `assumptions.md` already carries — and it is the single most testable pricing claim on this page.**
- 🔴🔴 **CONTRACT-LEVEL ECONOMICS, STATED TOGETHER FOR THE FIRST TIME BY THE COMPANY — MARGIN, PAYBACK AND THE REVENUE/CAPEX RATIO.**
  - **EBITDA margin:** *"contract level… EBITDA margins… being in the **mid 70s for a five-year deal and meaningfully higher for a shorter term deal**"*, with **five-year as *"the floor… from a price per megawatt perspective."***
  - **Payback:** *"depending on contract duration and prepayments terms, because **most of our deals do have a prepayment attached to them**, your payback period is… **as low as… truly a year, but approaching a year and as high as like three years** or something like that."*
  - **Revenue-to-capex:** *"historically, we've talked about like a **two to one ratio for a five-year deal** between revenue and capex. And… that was the answer probably **end of last year, early this year**. Whereas now… **contract terms have gotten more attractive and more favorable**. And so… **that relationship has expanded** since then."* ⚠️ **NO NEW RATIO IS GIVEN — the direction is stated, the level is not.**
  - ➤ **AGAINST WHAT THIS PAGE ALREADY HOLDS (DB unit economics, 16 Jun 2025, § Unit economics above): contract EBITDA margin ~80% in the company's own illustrative example (DB framework 78%; range 65-90%), payback ~2.5 yrs incl. prepayment, and ~$0.44 of annual revenue per $1 of capex over an initial 4-yr contract (≈1.8:1 over four years, ≈2.2:1 read across five). ⚠️ NOT A SUPERSESSION — different source, different contract shape, fifteen months apart — but note the shape of the difference: the MARGIN mark is lower (mid-70s vs ~80%) while the PRICE and the revenue/capex ratio are said to be higher. Those move in opposite directions and the source does not reconcile them. The candidate explanation the page should test is Vera Rubin capex inflation (Arete: VR NVL72 racks ~70-80% more than Blackwell, ~20% more GPU capex per MW) eating margin even as price rises — which is the same arithmetic Arete used to argue the OPPOSITE (5-10ppt of margin EXPANSION on new VR business).**
- 🔴 **CAPACITY AND FOOTPRINT — WITH THE BASIS STATED, BECAUSE THIS PAGE CARRIES THREE DIFFERENT POWER DENOMINATORS.** *"more than 1.5 gigawatts [of] power we have online today"* and *"more than 50 data centers now active"* / *"more than 50 data centers online already. And that number will continue to grow across the **US, Canada, Europe, and Asia**."* ⚠️⚠️ **THIS IS ONLINE/ACTIVE POWER — the same basis as the 1.5 GW active reported at the Q2 print (08-11), NOT the ~4.2 GW CONTRACTED and NOT a facility/gross-MW figure. Nothing about contracted power was updated on this call.** ✅ **Consistent with, not additive to, the Q2 disclosure — but it is the first company confirmation that >50 data centres are ACTIVE (the ~50 count on this page came from Hedgeye's model).** ➤ **And the siting strategy is stated as a deliberate contrast to the mega-campus model: *"We are not the platform that has really been excited to rush to building a two gigawatt, five gigawatt, eight gigawatt campus in one place"* — his answer to whether efficient inference needs geographic breadth is *"a little bit of both,"* pointing at the distributed map.**
- 🔴🔴 **MANAGED INFERENCE GETS ITS FIRST NUMBER, AND IT IS THE ONLY QUANTIFICATION OF THE MOVE-UP-THE-STACK STORY BESIDES THE ATTACH RATE:** the product *"we launched only a month or two ago, but is very, or I guess now two or three months ago… has grown very quickly **from one to more than 100 million of booked ARR** in that very short time period."* ⚠️⚠️ **"BOOKED ARR" IS A BOOKINGS MEASURE, NOT REVENUE: no definition, no reconciliation to any reported line, company-stated and unaudited, and on a base of ~$1m it is a near-infinite growth rate on a number that is still <1% of run-rate revenue. Do NOT model it.** ➤ **It matters anyway because it is the first falsifiable marker on the inference-layer product, and it sits directly under the internal-demand-sink argument Intrator used on 09-08 to answer the hyperscaler-retrenchment bear.** ✅ **ATTACH RATE RESTATED, NOT NEW — *"more than 75 percent of our customers consume at least three of our products"* (already on this page from 09-08 and 09-14). NEW COLOUR: the sub-25% are *"often not the really big lab or hyperscaler, but actually the very small Neo Lab who's just getting started. And so they just need GPUs, but they will become a multi-product customer over time."* That is the opposite composition from what a bear would assume.**
- 🔴🔴 **CAPITAL STRUCTURE — THE DIRECT MANAGEMENT ANSWER TO THE 09-17 AND 09-20 FINANCING BLOCKS BELOW, AND HE VOLUNTEERS IT UNPROMPTED TWICE.**
  - **THE ATM IS NOT IN USE AND CANNOT BE:** *"I want to be very, very clear. **We are not in the market using the ATM program right now.** In fact, **we are locked up on it** and we won't actually be in position between a lockup and blackout period for earnings. We won't be in position to even think about using the ATM program **until after our next quarter earnings call**."* Rationale for filing it at all: *"you got to do it at some point in time. Show a willingness to ultimately sell equity at some point in time. But to be very clear **it is not right now and it is not at these prices**"* — and *"our expectation is that would be **at a fundamentally different level to where our stock price is today**."*
  - **THE CONVERTIBLE IS CAPPED:** *"we actually bought a cat[c]all that pushed the **effective strike price of the convertible up to about $200**. So we're **staving off dilution** on that convertible until our stock price is **north of $200 let alone north of $100**."* ⚠️ *Cap-call economics are a hedge purchased with cash, not free dilution protection; no premium cost is disclosed here.*
  - **THE FUNDING SOURCE HIERARCHY, AND THE FALSIFIABLE CLAIM:** *"**DDTLs are going to remain and will remain for quite some time our primary source of capital**, but we will occasionally be in the market raising parent capital as well."* And then: *"we're at the point in time now where… in order to be able to look folks in the eye and say, **we feel like we have now accessed the capital required at the parent level to fund our 2026 expected CapEx plan**. We had one more raise to do and waiting for the market to get better or continuing to get worse didn't feel like the right approach."* ➤➤ **That is management saying the ~$6.4bn equity-leaning package (09-17) and the $3.7bn convert that priced above target (09-20) COMPLETE the 2026 parent-level funding need. It is dated, specific and testable at the next print — and it is the first thing on this page that answers the "how much more equity is coming" question the 09-17 Barclays note opened.**
  - **AND THE COST SIDE OF THE SHORT-DATED PREMIUM, WHICH THIS PAGE HAS NOT BEEN NETTING:** *"while pricing power is much higher or price performance is much higher on shorter dated deals, **cost of financing is also higher and depth of financing is lower**"*, and *"**financing shorter term is way, way, way more expensive and way more dilutive to our shareholders** than financing longer term contracts."* ⚠️ **Read the ~$40M/MW short-dated mark and the [[NBIS]] +20% price rise against this: the incremental margin on short-dated business is NOT the price delta.**
  - **NON-RECOURSE PAPER HAS A CREDIT FLOOR, MARKET-WIDE:** asked whether CRWV would issue non-recourse DDTLs for non-investment-grade clients — *"that's going to be pretty challenging, **not just for CoreWeave, but for the market** in the near term… **non-recourse will probably be reserved for investment grade customers for the entirety of the market**, not just for CoreWeave at this point in time."* ➤ **A structural ceiling on how far the AI-native/enterprise diversification can go before it changes the funding mix — the financing-side mirror of the customer-concentration debate below.**
- 🔴🔴 **THE SELF-BUILD SCHEDULE, SIZED FOR THE FIRST TIME ON THIS PAGE.** *"our **first self-built data center will come online later this year**, and our next will come online **next year in 27**. **The first one will be tens of megawatts. The second one will be 100 plus.** **By 2028**, I expect a more meaningful amount of our new capacity… will be self-built. And ultimately, over time, **it will become the majority of our new DCs**."* The gate is capital allocation, stated plainly: *"we've been forced to decide between allocating the incremental dollar to **owning the server and building scale** versus **buying the data center and vertically integrating**. And undeniably, **the higher ROI is on owning the server** and accelerating your scale. But as we get bigger and we don't have to decide between the two, we want to do both."* ⚠️ **BASIS: "tens of MW" and "100+ MW" carry NO stated gross-vs-IT-load basis — do not convert and do not add to any GW ladder.** ⚠️ **TENSION WITH THE 09-08 PRIMARY, FLAGGED NOT RESOLVED: Intrator said *"you're starting to see the first… 2 or 3 of our self builds… spin up"*; Robbins says the FIRST comes online later this year and the SECOND in 2027. "Spin up" and "come online" may be different construction stages — but one self-build in 2026 and two-to-three are not the same statement. Neither adopted.** ➤ **This also re-prices the vertical-integration margin story Intrator called "the lesser of the two" reasons on 09-08: on Robbins' schedule the owned-DC share of the fleet is immaterial before 2028, so no margin-stack benefit should be modelled into 2026-27.**
- 🔴 **PORTFOLIO CONSTRUCTION AND HELD-BACK CAPACITY — AND ONE ITEM HERE UPDATES A CITI RELAY THIS PAGE HAS CARRIED SINCE JULY.** *"I think **we're overweight long-term, underweight short-term today**. And we want to see those two things balance out, which means **you're going to see us sell a lot more short-term contracts** to offset kind of the big foundation we've built of long-term."* The mechanism by which that capacity appears: *"we have **capacity that's coming off their first contract that we fully own. We've repaid all of the debt. So it's fully unlevered**… And we get to go sell that into the short-dated market."* ✅ **That is the company-side confirmation of Arete's "near-term route to the new pricing is MATURING GPU CONTRACTS, mainly starting in 4Q26" (08-21).** 🔴 **AND THE HELD-BACK BOOK: *"we have intentionally held back… some meaningful amount of capacity that we've yet to contract out to maintain that flexibility. That's why we **still have some 2027 capacity left**. That's why we **haven't sold any of our capacity that begins to come online in 2028, even though we could sell it out all today if we wanted**."*** ➤ **Citi's 07-01 relay on this page said CRWV "still has a good amount of 2027 capacity to sell and they're allocating it based on the highest price." That is now confirmed by the company and extended: zero of 2028 is sold, deliberately. ⚠️ Unfalsifiable as stated — "we could sell it out all today" is an assertion, not a disclosure.**
- **PRICE STRUCTURE, ODDS AND ENDS — three answers that are small individually and useful as a set.** (1) **Term discount ladder:** *"There's **not much of a discount between a one-year deal and an on-demand contract**. There's a **bigger difference between a five-year deal and an on-demand contract**."* (2) **Pricing is two-way, and he says so against his own book:** *"it's not that price of on demand has only gone up and never gone down in the history here. **A year ago at this time, people were worried that prices were only going to go down.** And now people are focused on the fact that they've recently gone up a lot."* (3) **Fleet refresh is not cheap, which bears on the ~6yr depreciation debate:** converting an air-cooled Ampere/Hopper site to liquid-cooled Rubin *"would not only require buying new chips, but you would probably need to **decommission that data center for a year**, spend a lot more money… on **building in liquid cooling loops**, and then redeploying"* — so the hurdle is *"considerably more"* money, **which he explicitly declines to quantify** (*"I don't know that I have a numerical answer for that today"*).
- **DEMAND COMPOSITION — THE NAMED PARTNER ROSTER IS THE NET-NEW PART.** Model neutrality is a restatement (already on this page from 09-08 and 09-14), but the roster is not: **closed-source** — *"a key training and inference partner to OpenAI, to Anthropic, to Google, to Microsoft, to the next tier down of model companies like the Isomorphic Labs and the Periodic Labs and the Runways and the Mid Journeys… Cursor"*; **open-weight, *"particularly outside of China"*** — *"a direct training and inference partner for Meta, for Mistral, for Cohere, for NVIDIA."* And the margin claim attached to the first list: *"the contracts that we're signing with closed source companies… are **incredibly profitable and lucrative today**. And that's how we're able to access the financing at the scale that we have and the rates that we have been able to do so."* ⚠️ *Partner ≠ contracted customer ≠ disclosed backlog; none of these names is sized.*
- **A NEW DEMAND CATEGORY, NAMED BUT NOT YET SIZED: SAFETY / SECURITY / COMPLIANCE COMPUTE.** Regulatory and compliance demand *"we have seen… for a bit of time now"*; security and safety is *"certainly beginning"*, showing up two ways — customers *"going to experiment more and train and work with their models more before moving them out, which does require another increase in compute"*, and security vendors becoming cloud customers. Asked whether it is too early to treat as a separate demand category: *"I don't think so. But I do think it's going to be **meaningfully bigger a year from now** than it is today."* (The [[CRWD]] SafeMind datapoint from the same answer belongs on that page, not this one.)

**🆕🔴🔴 2026-09-17 — A ~$6.4BN FINANCING THAT LEANS EQUITY, AND — MORE IMPORTANTLY — THE FIRST PUBLIC PRICE POINT ON NEW SHORT-DATED COMPUTE CONTRACTS: ~$40MN/MW.** (Barclays · **Raimo Lenschow / Sheldon McMeans**, *"New Financing Leans Equity-Based but Pricing Commentary Is the Positive News to Focus on Here"*, **Equal Weight, PT $105**, price 09-16 **$83.35**, **2026-09-17**; company announcement same morning; tape via @zerohedge, 2026-09-17.)

- 🔴 **THE STRUCTURE: a proposed $3.0bn CONVERTIBLE SENIOR NOTES offering due 2033, PLUS a separate 35mn-share ATM EQUITY offering, plus an option for initial purchasers of up to an additional $500mn of principal. At 09-16 prices Barclays sizes the total at ~$6.4bn — ~$3.5bn from the convert plus option and ~$2.9bn from the ATM.**
- ⚠️ **WHY IT IS A QUESTION AND NOT JUST A RAISE: "These announcements lean MORE EQUITY-BASED than previous financings, which we think could raise some investor questions on the PREFERRED FINANCING MIX AMID A HIGHER RATE ENVIRONMENT." Barclays' own answer is that it "reflects DEAL MIX more than a change in financing strategy" — an explanation, not evidence. The Fed hiked 25bp on 09-16 with hawkish Warsh commentary (MS ECON now expects two more 25bp hikes, Dec and Mar, to 4.25-4.5%), so "higher rate environment" is the live backdrop, not a hypothetical.**
- 🔴🔴 **THE NUMBER TO CARRY: "CRWV noted new ST contracts signed at ~$40MN/MW, WHICH IS A CLEAR POSITIVE" — the company "continues to sign new compute capacity at HIGHER PRICES", on SHORT-DATED contracts.**
- ⚠️⚠️ **UNIT DISCIPLINE, MANDATORY BEFORE THIS NUMBER IS USED ANYWHERE ELSE ON THIS WIKI. There are now at least four different "$ per watt" bases circulating in this corpus and they are NOT interchangeable:**
  1. **~$40mn/MW — CRWV, short-dated contract pricing, 2026-09-17, basis (total contract value vs annualised) NOT STATED in the note.**
  2. **~$16mn/MW/YEAR — the 22V figure this wiki already carries, explicitly ANNUAL, from three deals.**
  3. **[[NVDA]]'s $18/25/40bn of REVENUE per GW.**
  4. **~$30-60bn of BUILD COST per GW.**
  ➤ **Do NOT net (1) against (2) to claim a ~2.5x price increase until the contract TERM behind the $40mn is known. A 2-3 year short-dated contract quoted at total value would be roughly consistent with $16mn/MW/year and would imply no price increase at all; quoted annually it would be a very large one. The note does not say which, and Barclays' own framing ("continues to sign new compute capacity at higher prices") suggests a genuine rise but does not settle the base.**

AI Neoclouds Are Flexing Their PRICING POWER"** (@barronsonline, 2026-09-17), on the same day **[[NBIS]] raised GPU pricing ~+20%.** ➤ **Two neoclouds raising price in one session is the read-through that matters more than the capital structure: it is the first same-day, two-name evidence on this page that neocloud pricing power is a sector condition rather than a CRWV-specific negotiation.**

Both things can be true at once: the filter is real AND CoreWeave is not on the hyperscaler side of it.** ⚠️ **No names, no capacity, no timeframe. Logged as posture, not as a forecast.**

Open-weight substitution can leave total token demand intact while compressing the revenue per token the fleet earns, and nothing in the session addressed that second leg.** **Consistent with what the page already holds: Omni installs CoreWeave software on infrastructure owned by enterprises or partners, including jurisdictions where CoreWeave will not own assets — one deal done, a whole bunch in the pipeline, no values given.**

**🆕🔴🔴 2026-09-09 (/run-inbox) — THE PRIMARY TRANSCRIPT OF INTRATOR AT GS COMMUNACOPIA IS NOW ON THE PAGE, AND THE MOST INVESTABLE LINE IN IT IS A FUNGIBILITY CLAIM: COREWEAVE SAYS IT DOES NOT SEGMENT ITS FLEET INTO TRAINING AND INFERENCE AT ALL.** (Goldman Sachs Communacopia + Technology Conference — **MANAGEMENT: Mike Intrator, CEO of CoreWeave**, in conversation with **Gabriela Borges**, Goldman Sachs · Bloomberg **LIVE TRANSCRIPT**, 2026-09-08)

- ⚠️⚠️ **SOURCE QUALITY — READ THIS BEFORE QUOTING ANYTHING BELOW. This is a Bloomberg LIVE (not FINAL) transcript and its ASR is badly broken on proper nouns: "coral reef", "Corey", "Cori", "Curaleaf", "Qawi", "Callie", "Koroivos", "core wave" and "Core, we've" all appear where the speaker said COREWEAVE; "Mike Andrada" is MIKE INTRATOR; "Katokopia" is COMMUNACOPIA. Quotes below are reproduced verbatim except that these proper-noun garbles are marked rather than silently repaired, and a FINAL transcript will supersede this one — re-read it when it lands.** ⚠️ **Two further items are flagged UNCERTAIN rather than corrected: the software product Intrator calls "Omni" (the name may be garbled), and a credit-rating phrase that came out as "we were able to get the the venture rated so that we were able to borrow at a minus credit" — the intended rating grade is not recoverable from the text, so NO rating is recorded on this page from it.**
- 🔴🔴 **THE FUNGIBILITY CLAIM, VERBATIM, AND IT BEARS DIRECTLY ON THIS PAGE'S INFERENCE-MIGRATION BEAR:** *"we don't really think about our infrastructure as being, oh, this is our training infrastructure and this is our inference infrastructure. We think of it as AI infrastructure."* Asked why decisions optimised for training should also win in post-training and inference, Intrator's answer is that the fleet was deliberately built for **optionality** because "the space was going to evolve very, very quickly", to "a standard that allows our clients to use that infrastructure in whatever way they deem to be most… productive for their businesses"; **Mission Control and "all the other… observability suites"** are named as the tooling layer that makes it work. He concedes only that "the consumption profile will look different" by use case and that "different parts of our stack will provide for different advantages". ⚠️ **THIS IS MANAGEMENT'S CLAIM AND IT IS NOT INDEPENDENTLY VERIFIED — CoreWeave discloses no utilisation or revenue split by workload, so there is nothing in-corpus to test it against. Logged because the standing bear that inference migrates to cheaper silicon implicitly assumes a training-specific fleet; if the fleet really is fungible, that bear needs a different mechanism.**
- 🔴 **THE PERFORMANCE ARGUMENT, WHICH IS THE ANSWER TO "HOW DID A CRYPTO MINER GET HERE": BUILT FROM FIRST PRINCIPLES AS AN AI-SPECIFIC CLOUD RATHER THAN A RETROFIT.** Borges opens by saying third-party data and her own customer conversations "consistently" report CoreWeave delivering GPU-training performance "that is unparalleled in the industry". Intrator: the company had "an opportunity to… break it down to first principles and say, if you were going to build a cloud… specifically built to address the needs of artificial intelligence, parallelized computing, how would you go about building that? And the clear answer was, you don't go about doing that by retrofitting a legacy environment." The internal shorthand is *"the minivan versus the F1"*; what compounds is "these compounding, incremental… thousands of decisions that have been built around… making the environment as performant as possible for this specific use case" — plus, explicitly, **client selection**: "we select our clients carefully… in terms of finding folks that need this use case to be able to be supported." ➤ **Note what this is and is not: a qualitative moat story with no benchmark, no utilisation figure and no price attached, and the third-party performance validation inside it is BORGES'S, not the company's.**
- 🔴 **SUPPLY/DEMAND — THE "END OF THE DECADE" FRAMING IS RE-PUT TO HIM AND HE CONFIRMS CONSISTENCY WITHOUT RESTATING THE DATE. Precision matters here: it is BORGES who attributes the date to the company** — "Cori [CoreWeave] has consistently said that we might not be at supply demand equilibrium until the end of the decade." Intrator's reply endorses the consistency and the direction, not a date: "we have been incredibly consistent… in the way that we talk about demand", and **"we have never flinched from our assessment that… the world's capacity to deliver compute has been and will continue to be wildly overwhelmed by the… delivery and demand for intelligence, broadly speaking. And so… we've been righter than wrong on that one."** ⚠️ **Do NOT log an end-of-decade equilibrium date as a company statement from this transcript — it is the analyst's characterisation of prior CoreWeave statements, confirmed only in spirit.**
- 🔴 **DEMAND BREADTH, AND ONE NEW PIECE OF COLOUR: THE "NINE MONTH RULE".** The cross-section he claims to see spans "the hyperscalers through the AI natives, the… frontier labs… people that are productizing and delivering agentic solutions". Two dynamics: (1) existing large consumers "are demanding ever larger amounts of resources" — *"we have the nine month rule at core [CoreWeave]. We… look back at whatever we've done and… nine months later we think it's cute"*; (2) compounding on top of that, **new entrants that are ENTERPRISES building their own clusters** — "a company like Caterpillar comes and starts to use ours… the way that we're going to train our models, the way that we're going to serve our models is going to look different than it has historically. We're going to build our own clusters." He calls that "transformational" and the thing "allowing CoreWeave to establish itself as an AI cloud participant… a hyperscaler within the AI system". ✅ **Caterpillar is already on this page from the Q2 call — what is new is the WHY: twenty years of cloud experience left enterprises wanting control over a resource they now consider fundamental ("the boiled lobster problem… this time, they're going in with eyes wide open"), and CoreWeave pitches itself as the alternative to "an oligopoly of… three massive companies".**
- **VERTICAL INTEGRATION INTO OWNED DATA CENTRES — TWO REASONS, AND MARGIN IS EXPLICITLY THE LESSER ONE.** Borges notes the first CoreWeave-owned data centre comes online by the end of this year and asks what drives it beyond margin stacking. Intrator: recapturing margin is real but "the lesser of the two"; the bigger reason is control and learning — "this stuff is difficult… entering into an environment where we have more control, a deeper understanding, a better sandbox to work on, the flywheel of new innovations that we want to bring to our own data centers. That makes us stronger." Status: **"you're starting to see the first… 2 or 3 of our self builds… spin up"**, with continued effort to "control the physical infrastructure both up and down the stack". ⚠️ **The end-of-year timing is the ANALYST'S framing in the question, not a management statement.**
- **DATA-CENTRE PERMITTING POLITICS INTO THE MIDTERMS — "AT MAXIMUM VOLUME", AND HIS POSITION IS THAT IT MOVES CAPACITY RATHER THAN CANCELLING IT.** He concedes parts of the backlash "warrant real introspection by the industry" and parts are "completely nonsense"; on water, "these are closed loop systems… they must be closed loop systems. They don't function unless they are." The investable line: **"the approach of… not allowing data centers to be built is kind of a fool's errand… because… the demand for the compute doesn't change. It just moves in terms of where it's going to be built and how much it's going to cost to build."** ✅ **RE-STATEMENT, NOT NEW: the "over a gigawatt of… power contracted… outside of the US" diversification point he repeats here is already on this page from the Q2 call (which also carried the 360MW Indonesia detail). Nothing superseded; the useful addition is the admission that the politics changes COST as well as location.**
- 🔴 **NET-NEW AND COMMERCIALLY MATERIAL: NVIDIA HAS DESIGNATED COREWEAVE'S SOFTWARE STACK A REFERENCE ARCHITECTURE, AND CRWV NOW SELLS THAT STACK ONTO OTHER PEOPLE'S INFRASTRUCTURE.** "Nvidia designated our software stack as a reference architecture" — which he reads as a quality validation — and the commercial consequence is a capital-light channel: providing third parties "access to our software solution on their infrastructure allows us to get some leverage on other balance sheets", including "companies that want to own their own infrastructure" and **"jurisdictions that we maybe are not comfortable owning or delivering… an asset heavy… solution to"**, which he says expands TAM. The product is named in the transcript as **"Omni"** ⚠️ *(name UNCERTAIN — this transcript garbles proper nouns; the spelling is not confirmed)*. Cadence: **"we closed a deal on that… a couple of months ago, and there's a whole bunch coming down the pipe."** ➤ **Watch item: an asset-LIGHT software line inside a business the market models as pure asset-heavy leasing. No revenue, margin or deal size was given.**
- 🔴🔴 **CAPITAL STRUCTURE — AND THIS IS WHERE MANAGEMENT AND THE OBSERVABLE CREDIT TAPE ON THIS PAGE DISAGREE.** Intrator's account: equity was never the route ("if your space X and you're valued at $2 trillion… you sell some equity… But if you're building organically like we were, the question is… how do you raise the capital"), so **the engine is SPV-level borrowing with look-through to the OFFTAKER'S credit** — "they were basically looking through and saying, okay, it's Microsoft on the other side of this transaction. The money is going to flow into the SPV and then pay back the debt" — plus convertibles at the parent company. He claims a sequence of firsts: "we did the first GPU transactions", and **"the last debt structure we did was the first time anybody has been willing to take… renewal risk"**, which he frames as the unlock for financing SHORT-TERM contracts — directly relevant to the roll-off/renewal question Agrawal declined to size on 08-11. ⚠️ **THE CLAIM TO TEST: "it puts our borrowing really at par or close to at par with many of the hyperscalers that are out there raising capital."** ➜ **The hardest datapoint on this page says otherwise: MS Global Credit Strategy (2026-08-20) prices the CRWV term loan (S+550, BB) at 9.15% and CoreWeave-offtake project bonds at 8.2–14.2% against Amazon-offtake bonds at 7.9–8.8% — a 300–600bp counterparty gap. Both can be partly true (the SPV look-through does borrow against the offtaker's credit, and CRWV's own loan tightened 57bp), but "at or close to par with the hyperscalers" is NOT what the tape shows. Logged as a divergence between management's characterisation and priced credit — not adopted.**
- **ATTACH RATE, RESTATED FROM A PRIOR CALL: "75% of our clients are using three different services… Three different silos within our product suite are already being consumed"** — he dates it to "maybe… two earnings ago" and argues the true figure is understated because early-stage clients have not yet reached products two and three. **On open vs closed weights CoreWeave is explicitly indifferent** ("more… demand for compute is better for our business"); **on world models it is "the same but different"** — the value proposition is the same infrastructure/software-rails argument, so no separate demand curve is claimed. **On industry structure, with new entrants announcing 7–10GW: "capital intensive industries will tend towards… periods of proliferation and they will tend towards periods of consolidation"**, and the gap that decides it is "the difference between launching a company and talking about a contract you've signed and actually being able to operationalize and deliver and maintain that infrastructure".
- ⚠️ **HOUSEKEEPING: this page's conference-week entry lists the 09-08 4:45PM ET GS Communacopia slot as the CFO's. The transcript in hand is the CEO's session. Either the roster changed or there were two slots — the existing line is left as logged, and this source is filed as MIKE INTRATOR, CEO.**

**🆕🔴🔴 2026-09-07 — THE BARGAINING POWER IN DATA-CENTRE LEASES HAS STARTED MOVING TOWARD THE OPERATOR, AND THE MECHANISM IS SLA TERMS.** (The Information · **Phoebe Liu**, "Desperation to Get Data Centers Online Is Reshaping Companies' Bargaining Power", 2026-09-07 — *reporting on unnamed data-centre and credit executives*)
- 🔴 **"The biggest cloud providers usually have had the upper hand in negotiating to rent capacity from newer clouds CORE WEAVE, NEBIUS and NSCALE… because the big companies have the most capital and best credit ratings. BUT THINGS ARE CHANGING: cloud providers like [[MSFT]] are SO DESPERATE to ensure the server racks they've ordered from [[NVDA]] can get working as quickly as possible that DATA CENTER OPERATORS NOW HAVE MORE POWER TO NEGOTIATE DOWN THE RISKS THEY SHOULDER."**
- 🔴 **What the old terms looked like, and they are worse than most models assume: "The starting point for the big cloud providers in many deal talks is to get the neocloud to commit to ensuring EVERY SERVER RACK IS RUNNING ALMOST 100% OF THE TIME"; temperature and humidity requirements "that can be ALMOST IMPOSSIBLY STRICT"; and one executive "has seen proposals where IF JUST ONE RACK WENT DOWN (due to a power failure, thermal overload, broken switches or other outage), THE BIG CLOUD COULD VOID SIX MONTHS OF RENT."** Sources add that **"SLA DEFAULTS ARE NOT UNCOMMON; CUSTOMERS USUALLY JUST DON'T ACT ON THEM."**
- ➤➤ **THIS IS THE MOST IMPORTANT DISCLOSURE-QUALITY POINT ON THE NEOCLOUD PAGES AND IT CUTS BOTH WAYS. (1) It says the contracted-revenue backlogs these names report are subject to penalty clauses of a severity that is NOT in any public model — "one rack down voids six months of rent" is a contingent liability, not a footnote. (2) It simultaneously says those clauses are ROUTINELY BREACHED AND NOT ENFORCED, which means the reported backlog has been economically real. 🔴 The investable change is the direction: operators are now trading PRICE for DURABILITY — "the more they can negotiate LESS PUNITIVE SLAs, the better, EVEN IF IT MEANS A LOWER PRICE." Expect contracted rates to look softer and contract QUALITY to improve. Do not read a lower headline lease rate as a demand signal without asking what happened to the SLA.**
- 🔴 **The other lever is chip-vendor credit: "NVIDIA and [[AMD]] have sometimes gotten in a BIDDING WAR over which one of them will PROVIDE CREDIT GUARANTEES to the customer of a particular data center… These potential deals BENEFIT DATA CENTER DEVELOPERS because Nvidia and AMD would provide credit support for a portion of the lease OVER 15 YEARS, rather than the existing SIX-YEAR deals Nvidia has disclosed with some neoclouds."**
- **And the cost side: "THE COST OF BUILDING OUT A ONE-GIGAWATT DATA CENTER HAS MORE THAN DOUBLED IN THE LAST FEW YEARS… it's EXCRUCIATINGLY DIFFICULT TO PREDICT how much a data center will cost."** Power is **"more (and sometimes much more) than a fifth of data centers' operating costs"**, with **"fluctuations of as much as 400% for POWER RATES for different customers in the same month."**
- ⚠️ **All of the above is anonymously sourced trade reporting. NOT ADOPTED into estimates. Logged because it is the first substantive account of the CONTRACT STRUCTURE beneath the backlog numbers, and because the direction of change is the opposite of the consensus assumption that hyperscaler credit dominates the negotiation.**

**🆕🔴🔴 2026-09-02 (ingest; source ~Sep) — SECURITY ENTERS THE NEOCLOUD SELECTION CRITERIA FOR THE FIRST TIME, AND SEMIANALYSIS FRAMES IT AS A **COUNTERPARTY** QUESTION: "SOME DESERVE THAT TRUST AND SOME REALLY DO NOT." **CLUSTERMAX 3 WILL "NAME AND SHAME"** — AN UNDATED BUT NEAR-TERM, DIFFERENTIATING CATALYST FOR THE WHOLE COMPLEX.**

_(Source: **SemiAnalysis — "Something Else Weekly" ep. 028**, *"Most Neoclouds Suck At Security: How Agents Hacked Hugging Face"*, with a companion article. Speakers **Doug O'Laughlin**, **Jordan** and **Sam** (surnames as rendered; the transcript garbles them). Firm confirmed internally: **ClusterMAX** is SemiAnalysis's own neocloud rating framework (*"these guys are the guys who do ClusterMAX"*, *"we're going to name and shame in ClusterMAX 3"*, the `pip install cluster-max` utility). Date not stated — they place Mythos's 7-Apr announcement at *"5 to 7 months to the present day"* => **~Sep 2026**; ingested 2026-09-02. **SemiAnalysis is a CONTINUOUS SOURCE on this wiki (own daily lane in /newsletters-to-vault) — a research shop, NEVER a broker: no ratings, no PTs, no models.** WARNING: machine transcript — "cyberjim"~CyberGym, "graphana"=Grafana, "arbback"=RBAC, "Kimmy"=Kimi. ⚠️ **The NSA/CIA-honeypot riff about obliteration.ai is an EXPLICIT ON-AIR JOKE ("this is a joke to be clear") and is NOT recorded here as a claim.**)_

🔴 **READ-DISCIPLINE: they sell this.** ClusterMAX is their commercial product and **ClusterMAX 3 is promoted in this episode**, so the "neoclouds are insecure" framing is self-interested. **But the single most important finding below — that they went looking for evidence of AI transforming cybersecurity practice and FOUND ALMOST NOTHING — runs against their own interest and is explicitly hedged as mid-analysis. Weight accordingly: highest on the null result and the technical forensics, normal discount on the product pitch.**

- 🔴🔴 **THE FRAMING, AND IT IS AN INVESTABLE ONE: "it's a COUNTERPARTY, right? When startups are raising 60, 70, 80% of their venture capital and spending it on GPUs, they are really putting a lot of faith and trust in these neocloud providers — and SOME DESERVE IT and SOME REALLY DO NOT deserve that trust when it comes to security."** They report **huge variability**: some neoclouds *"are getting to the point where they operate like hyperscalers"* — and **hyperscalers set the bar** *"because they've been serving everybody from top Fortune 500 companies to federal governments all around the world and they've been held to this standard for a while"* — while others are *"years out of date on software before anybody's really started talking about this thing, just sitting there waiting to be exploited,"* with **no patching process and no customer-notification process**, despite serving *"banking, telco, research, academia, federal defence and national intelligence at large, top-10-global-GDP countries."* ➤ **ClusterMAX has rated neoclouds on uptime, reliability and SLA; this adds SECURITY as a rating axis for the first time — which matters for CRWV and [[NBIS]] precisely because it is a dimension where hyperscaler-grade operators can DIFFERENTIATE against the long tail instead of competing on price. Watch which tier each lands in.**
- 🔴 **THE CATALYST, DATED ONLY AS "COMING SOON": "we're going to NAME AND SHAME in ClusterMAX 3."** They also disclose that earlier rounds were not comparable — providers tested in *"March or April didn't get the same level of tender loving care when it came to security"* as later ones, because the test suite was built as they went. ➤ **So ClusterMAX 3 is the first apples-to-apples security ranking, and it is explicitly intended to embarrass laggards: treat publication as event risk for any listed neocloud and a potential differentiator for the leaders.** ⚠️ **ClusterMAX is SemiAnalysis's commercial product and this episode is partly promotion for it — the ranking is their opinion, not an audit standard.**
- 🔴 **THE FINDINGS CATALOGUE, because the specifics are what make this credible rather than rhetorical.** From hands-on testing: a **cross-tenant remote code execution** at an Asian provider — *"I was able to write something into the logs in one of the tenants and read it from the others"* — where **the other tenants were inference providers on OpenRouter**, so *"anybody's OpenClaw or coding assistant using this provider's endpoint on OpenRouter is conceptually exposing credentials"*; **BMCs left open to the public internet**; **front-end networking with no VLAN/VXLAN**, giving cross-tenant visibility; **missing InfiniBand security keys** (P/M/SA keys) on the back-end fabric; **storage servers not enforcing RBAC** across volumes and users; **customers able to reach the storage underlay** from the overlay network; **multi-tenant Grafana scoped by a single auth token over a god-level Prometheus token** (*"you need Grafana per instance — you can't do multi-tenant Grafana, that is crazy"*); and **container/VM breakout to root where there was only ONE isolation layer** on shared servers. ➤ **Their design rule is the takeaway: NO SINGLE POINTS OF FAILURE — no single auth token, no single isolation boundary; "have multiple layers, so if somebody breaks out of a container they're still in a VM."**
- ✅ **AND THE BAR IS LOW, WHICH IS THE GENUINELY REASSURING PART FOR QUALITY OPERATORS: "the stuff we're calling out here is NOT anything galaxy[-brain]. We're not interested in you inventing novel cryptography techniques. Just DO THE BASICS CORRECTLY — make sure tenants are properly isolated, follow all the very basic security best practices, and everyone will be much better off."** Their headline prescription is simply **"keep your stuff up to date,"** since an agent can read a public vulnerability description and build an exploit from it. They have shipped a free checker — **`pip install cluster-max` then `cmax audit security`** — updated by GitHub Action against NVIDIA, Docker, Linux-kernel and AMD security bulletins, covering GPU drivers, NIC firmware and **BlueField DPUs** (*"which have their own little CPU on them that you can control"*). ➤ **Useful diligence tool: a customer can run it against a prospective provider's cluster from the TENANT side. They report providers have already begun patching in response — "from people that you'd be surprised to hear."**
- ⚠️ **ONE UNVERIFIED ASIDE FLAGGED ONLY BECAUSE OF WHO IT INVOLVES: the hosts refer in passing to "Hugging Face's acquisition from [[NVDA]]" potentially going "up in smoke" over the incident — i.e. they speak of an NVDA acquisition of Hugging Face as a live or done fact.** Said mid-way through an extended joking riff, with no sourcing and no detail. **NOT recorded as a deal datapoint — noted purely so it can be checked against primary sources, since an NVDA-Hugging Face transaction would be material and appears nowhere else on this wiki.**

**🆕 2026-08-25 — TWO ITEMS, ONE TECHNICAL AND ONE COMPETITIVE.**
- **Benchmark provenance:** SemiAnalysis's comparison of [[OPENAI]]'s Jalapeño against Vera Rubin uses **"Vera Rubin's latest July 2026 results" published by NVIDIA and CoreWeave** as the reference point — *"these are the best public Rubin numbers"* — and notes those figures came from **CoreWeave engineering samples that "are still far from optimized"** (SemiAnalysis, 2026-08-25). 🔴 **CRWV is the venue through which the industry's public Rubin performance data is being produced, which is a genuine early-access signal — and simultaneously the reason the comparison flatters Jalapeño, since immature software is being compared to immature software.**
- **Tape:** *"**NBIS +5% continues to act a lot better than CRWV +2% post-earnings. The latter has FILLED ITS EARNINGS GAP COMPLETELY while NBIS is still way above it**"* (TMTB EOD Wrap, 2026-08-25). **And the sector framing that moved the group on 08-24: "the companies that can deal with states and local governments thrived and the NEOCLOUDS WITHOUT THE CLOUT OR THE BUCKS GOT CRUSHED — NEW PATTERN"** (@jimcramer, corpus, 2026-08-25). ⚠️ **Commentary, not analysis — logged because siting/permitting capability is now being priced as a neocloud differentiator, which is a new variable on this page.**
- **The financing backdrop that surrounds the whole cohort:** MS's NVDA credit initiation puts **"over $1 trillion of guided GPU/XPU-related ecosystem financing… via creative structures"** and **~$170bn of NVDA contingent obligations across leases, PORTS-Pike, the $500bn RVS and revenue-sharing**; the WSJ separately reports NVDA carrying *"up to $230bn in lease backstops and residual-value guarantees… plus **customer-of-last-resort deals and equity stakes in smaller cloud players**"* (MS · Lindsay Tyler; WSJ via JPM, both 2026-08-25).

**🆕🔴 2026-08-14 — TWO POST-PRINT DATAPOINTS THAT GO STRAIGHT AT THIS PAGE'S TWO STANDING BEARS (pricing power and asset obsolescence), AND THEY COME FROM A DESK RATHER THAN FROM THE COMPANY.** (MS Sales · Skyler Risom, "Friday Five," 2026-08-14, relaying Adam Jonas. ⚠️ **Sales commentary, explicitly not MS Research. Both figures are the desk's characterisation of the Q2 print/call — cross-check against the primary transcript already on this page before modelling.**)

**🆕🔴 2026-08-24 — COREWEAVE JUST SUPPLIED THE SINGLE BEST PUBLIC DATAPOINT IN THE GPU-RESIDUAL-VALUE DEBATE, AND IT IS ABOUT A SIX-YEAR-OLD CHIP.**
- **The Information (Dakin Campbell, Special Report, 2026-08-24), in a piece about whether GPUs can serve as loan collateral:** *"Nvidia-backed neocloud CoreWeave, a major buyer of GPUs, recently chimed in on this idea, saying **it had signed a contract for A100s, a chip first introduced in 2020, that extends into 2029**."*
- 🔴 **That is a ~9-year revenue life on a 2020 part, asserted by the operator. It is the direct counter to the lenders' central objection, stated in the same piece: _"Many people expect GPUs to depreciate quickly, in part because of how quickly Nvidia has introduced newer chips with better performance."_ If the A100 contract is representative, CRWV's depreciation schedule — the most contested line in its P&L on this page — is conservative rather than aggressive.** ⚠️ **One contract, no disclosed rate, no disclosed volume, and self-reported by the party with the strongest incentive to make exactly this point. Treat it as the best available anecdote, not as a fleet-level useful-life estimate.**
- **The lenders' unresolved counter-argument, verbatim from the same reporting:** GPU performance *"can vary widely based on factors such as **power supply, how they are networked, and what optimization software is used.** That worries lenders who would prefer a standard chip with a long life that can act as stable collateral."* ➜ 🔴 **The objection is not that GPUs die — it is that they are NOT FUNGIBLE in practice, against Huang's blog-post claim that software and networking make them "fungible." An A100 running in a well-networked, well-powered CRWV cluster is a different asset from the same die elsewhere, which is precisely why a residual-value market is hard to build.**
- 🔴 **A DATED, FALSIFIABLE FORWARD EVENT FOR THIS WHOLE DEBATE: "CME Group, in partnership with Silicon Data, hopes to launch FUTURES CONTRACTS for RENTAL PRICES of Nvidia's H100 and B200 chips in OCTOBER."** Ornn and Silicon Data already publish index-form forward curves. *"Such derivatives could let lenders offset some risk by taking short positions that gain value if GPU rental prices fall."* ⚠️ Caveat carried from the source: the underlying financing market likely has to exist before a liquid hedge market can. **Either way, an October listing is checkable — and a public H100/B200 rental forward curve would mark CRWV's fleet economics to a screen for the first time.**
- **Ownership/circularity, newly quantified:** NVDA's public-equity stakes rose from **$18.4bn (Mar-31) to $63.0bn (Jun-30)**, with the step-up attributed partly to *"increases in the value of stakes in companies such as [[INTC]] and CoreWeave"* — and the neutral framing that *"the investments buttress the financial strength of buyers of its chips (that's obviously true for CoreWeave and [[NBIS]])"* (The Information · Martin Peers, 2026-08-24, citing NVDA filings).
- 🔴 **PRICING: "CRWV ~25% PRICE INCREASE ACROSS SKUs indicates pricing power."** ➜ **If accurate this is the most direct answer yet to the "neocloud is a commodity rental business with no pricing power" bear. It also lines up with the independent tracker evidence the same week — UBS/Evidence Lab has Blackwell "supply remains tight with pricing going higher," with B300 available on-demand at only three vendors (AWS, Oracle, Nebius) and access otherwise "largely reserved or sales allocated" (UBS · Arcuri, 2026-08-14).** ⚠️ **The counter-signal in that same tracker: the only observable B300 SPOT series (AWS) decelerated from +28% MoM in June to +3% in July. A list-price rise and a decelerating spot print are not contradictory — contract and spot are different markets — but they are the two halves to watch.**
- 🔴 **ASSET LIFE: "contracts out to 2029 for NVDA chips from 2020 (AKA NINE-YEAR USEFUL LIFE!) can alleviate obsolescence concerns on AI infra."** ➜ **This is the depreciation-schedule debate that runs across [[NVDA]], [[AMZN]] and [[META]] on this wiki, answered here with a contract fact rather than an accounting assumption: a 2020-vintage part still contracted in 2029.** ⚠️ **One cohort, and the desk does not give the price at which those 2020 chips are contracted — a nine-year life at a collapsing rental rate is a very different asset from a nine-year life at a flat one. The Michael Burry critique already on the [[NVDA]] page (depreciation as an economic lever, not a physical measure) applies symmetrically here.** Independently consistent: **UBS's tracker finds Hopper/Ampere availability and pricing "holding up well… as it appears cloud vendors still see economic value in legacy chips."**
- **The $/watt marks the desk uses for CRWV, recorded with an explicit unit warning:** *"Adam's model uses a **weighted ~$31/watt in 4Q26 which collapses to ~$17/watt in 4Q27 on 5GW of year-end compute**. After CRWV print, it could make more sense to **run $31/watt out to 2027, which would 3x y/y revs (vs 2x y/y currently in the model)**. Of course, **GOOGL rented compute at $50/watt**."* ⚠️⚠️ **THESE ARE REVENUE-per-watt figures, NOT capex. They must NOT be netted against the $/GW build-cost anchors in `_meta/assumptions.md`. And note they are not comparable to each other: ~$31/$17 is a blended neocloud rental rate, $50 is a single Google deal, and the "15-30 per watt" circulating on [[GOOG]] this week is Alphabet's TPU SELL price to third parties. Three different quantities, same unit label.** ➜ **The tradeable content is the SLOPE, not the level: the base model assumes the blended rate roughly halves within a year as capacity scales. Holding $31 flat is what turns 2x into 3x revenue growth — so "does the rate hold?" is the single biggest swing factor in MS's CRWV revenue line.**

**🆕🔴 2026-08-12 (/run-inbox) — THE FULL PRIMARY TRANSCRIPT OF THE Q2 CALL IS NOW ON THE PAGE (Bloomberg FINAL TRANSCRIPT, "CoreWeave Inc Q2 2026 Earnings Call", 2026-08-11; participants Michael Intrator, CEO and Nitin Agrawal, CFO — MANAGEMENT commentary, not a broker note). ⚠️ PARTIAL DUPLICATE: the print itself and the four broker reads below are already on the page and STAND. Everything here is what the desk coverage did not carry — and the below-the-line items are the ones that matter, because this page's standing bear is financing, not demand.**

- **🔴 THE INTEREST LINE, WHICH NO DESK NOTE CARRIED AND WHICH IS THE BEAR'S EXHIBIT: Q2 interest expense $640M vs $267M in Q2'25 — up 140% y/y — and Q3 interest expense is GUIDED TO $860–940M.** Against Q2 adjusted operating income of **$128M**, interest is running **~5x** operating profit. Net loss **$(626)M** (vs $(290)M PY); adjusted net loss **$(567)M** (vs $(130)M PY). Q2 opex **$2.6B including $165M of SBC**. ⚠️ **Put this next to the celebrated margin inflection: adj. operating income actually FELL year-over-year ($128M vs $200M in Q2'25) even as it rose sequentially from $21M. The "operating leverage inflection" is a SEQUENTIAL story, not a year-over-year one, and the interest line is growing faster than the operating line. Neither JPM, DB, Barclays nor Bernstein put this in their same-day takeaways.**
- **✅ THE OFFSETTING FINANCING FACT, also net-new: CRWV raised ~$18B in Q2 alone across debt, convertibles and equity, taking total debt+equity capital secured to date to >$32B — and "over the past year, we have REDUCED OUR WEIGHTED-AVERAGE COST OF DEBT BY ALMOST 300 BASIS POINTS, representing approximately $1.1B of ANNUALIZED INTEREST SAVING based on our end of Q2 debt load."** Firsts inside that: an **inaugural eurobond**, the **first delayed-draw term loan backed by HPC infrastructure issued in the public markets**, and **DDTL 5.5 as the first to include shorter-duration customer contracts** — priced, per Agrawal, **"during one of the most dislocated weeks for credit this year"** and completed at full size, with **"our spreads have retraced since."** Cash, cash equivalents, restricted cash and marketable securities **>$6.9B** at 30-Jun. ⚠️ **The ~300bp/$1.1bn figure is the direct, quantified answer to the Rothschild/Bernstein "structurally disadvantaged by fixed debt" bear and to the SemiAnalysis 5.62%→10% funding-cost sensitivity already on this page — and it is management's number, not a broker's.**
- **🔴 BACKLOG QUALITY, disclosed for the first time and more useful than the headline: "of the existing backlog, MORE THAN 50% IS ATTACHED TO A CONTRACT WHERE CUSTOMER DELIVERY HAS COMMENCED. We expect this figure to reach MORE THAN TWO-THIRDS of our Q2 backlog by the end of this year."** ⚠️ **This is the metric that answers Vital Knowledge's "backlog didn't ramp much" bear on its own terms — the question was never only how big the backlog is, but how much of it is contracted-but-not-started. Half of $104B is already in delivery.**
- **🔴 CONSTRUCTION IN PROGRESS $11.9B, up from $9.6B q/q — Agrawal explicitly flags it as the forward indicator: it "signal[s] the significant amount of PP&E we expect to deploy early in Q3 based on the large amount of power we received very late in Q2." And the June concentration is starker than the quarterly number suggests: "in June, we brought on MORE THAN 300 MEGAWATTS of active power, which makes JUNE ITSELF LARGER THAN ANY FULL-QUARTER IN OUR HISTORY."**

**🆕🔴 2026-08-12 — THE MORNING AFTER: STOCK +19%, AND THE SELL-SIDE CONVERGED ON A SINGLE NUMBER THAT WAS NOT IN THE RELEASE — CONTRACTS SIGNED IN Q2 CARRY CONTRIBUTION MARGINS 5–10 POINTS ABOVE THOSE ADDED IN RECENT QUARTERS, ON PRICE INCREASES OF ~25% ON AN APPLES-TO-APPLES BASIS ACROSS SKUs.** This is the datapoint that re-rated the whole neocloud cohort, and it is the answer to this page's standing "are the economics real or just backlog?" debate.

- **🔴 PRICING — the net-new hard number (JPM / Samik Chatterjee, 2026-08-12):** **price increases of ~25% on average across SKUs for new contracts**, driven **primarily by the demand environment and accelerating customer ROI, with component cost pass-through only a SECONDARY factor**. ⚠️ **Read the causality carefully: management is explicitly saying this is demand-led pricing, not memory/component inflation being passed through. If that holds, it is margin, not a cost recovery.**
- **🔴 CONTRIBUTION MARGIN — "5 to 10 percentage points" above prior contracts**, in reflection of the demand backdrop **and technology leadership on new platforms including Vera Rubin** (JPM, 2026-08-12). DB puts the base under it: **new-deal contribution margins are 5–10pts above the prior mid-20s average and "likely continue to improve in 3Q"** (DB / Brad Zelnick, 2026-08-12) ➜ **i.e. roughly mid-20s ➜ ~30–35% on the new cohort.**
- **🔴 THE A100 CONTRACT — the single most quoted line of the day, and it is a duration/residual-value datapoint, not a pricing one:** *"As an example, we recently signed an **A100 contract that extends into 2029** at an attractive price. As a reminder, **this SKU was introduced in 2020**. … Increasingly, we are seeing **longer utilization at higher prices**, offering the potential for significant further upside"* (CRWV Q2'26 call, 2026-08-11, quoted in TMT Breakout EOD Wrap, 2026-08-12). ⚠️ **This is the most direct evidence yet against the depreciation-schedule bear this page carries: a 2020-vintage SKU contracting out to 2029 is a ~9-year useful life on the oldest generation in the fleet.**
- **DDTL 5.5 — JPM calls it "the single most important development":** the closing of **DDTL 5.5** lays out a path to **leverage asset-backed financing for customer contracts SHORTER in duration than the prior typical 5 years**, which (a) **expands the addressable customer set** to groups that want shorter contracts, (b) lets CRWV **participate in the premium pricing associated with shorter contracts**, and (c) **diversifies the customer base** (JPM, 2026-08-12). ⚠️ **This is the structural unlock: the financing structure, not demand, was what forced 5-year contracts — and 5-year contracts were what forced the concentration this page tracks in `## Customer concentration`.**
- **POWER — the numbers all four houses lead with:** **~500MW of active power added in the quarter (~300MW in June alone)**, taking **active power to ~1.5GW** vs Street ~1.25GW and ~1GW at Q1; **year-end active power guided up to >1.85GW from >1.7GW**; **total contracted power ~3.7GW at end-Q2 (from ~3.5GW at Q1), and 4.2GW as of early August**; **1.5GW landed power** ➜ JPM frames it as **visibility into 5.7GW against the 8GW-by-2030 target** (JPM, 2026-08-12; Barclays / Raimo Lenschow and DB / Zelnick corroborate the 4.2GW and >1.85GW marks).
- **CAPEX — record, and read as a positive:** **$9.35–9.4B adj. capex in Q2** vs JPMe $8.51B / consensus $8.0B / guide $7.0–9.0B; **3Q capex guided $11.5–13.5B** vs JPMe $10.9B / Street $10.4B; **FY26 capex raised to $35–39B from $31–35B**. DB: the $4B midpoint raise reflects **"an acceleration of assets placed into service, NOT a change in cost to deliver."** Barclays: *"Looking at Q2 from a classic ST-biased hedge fund lens that cares about revenue beat levels (only 0.5%) is missing the point this quarter… **the company added a record $9.4bn in adj. CapEx, which seems to be coming at 5-10pts of higher contribution margin.** Since CapEx speaks to future revenue (and now with a better margin profile), we think investors should be excited."*
- **⚠️ THE ONE MISS, and every house explains it the same way:** **3Q PF operating margin guided to 6.5% at the midpoint vs consensus 7.3% / JPMe 7.0%** — attributed to **capacity landing late in the quarter** (capacity came on "very late" in Q2 and hence could not contribute to revenue; the same dynamic pushes into 3Q). PF operating income itself guided **$200–260M vs JPMe $236M / Street $253M — below on margin %, above in absolute dollars.** Q2 PF operating income **$128M / 5.0% margin** vs guide $30–90M (2.4% mid), JPMe $67M, Street $66M — **a large beat on the metric investors said mattered most.**
- **PLATFORM PROOF POINTS — DB's "most important takeaway," and it is the anti-"GPU-as-a-Service" argument (DB / Zelnick, 2026-08-12):** (1) **customer base broadening into industrials, life sciences, financial services and AI natives** — *"not just a handful of hyperscalers and AI labs searching for scarce compute"*; (2) **AI products beyond GPUs (storage, CPU, networking, software) now >$400M of ARR in Q2**, margin-accretive; (3) **managed inference booked ARR >$100M just months post-launch, guided to exit the year >$250M**; (4) **first Omni deal signed, scaling in 2027** — DB calls it *"a capital-light growth vector with more SW-like margins ahead."*
- **Guide raised across the board:** FY26 revenue **$12.4–13.2B** (from $12.0–13.0B); **FY26 exit ARR raised $500M at the midpoint to $18.5–19.5B** (from $18.0–19.0B); **FY26 PF operating income $960M–$1,150M / 8.2% mid** (from $900M–1,100M / 8.0%), with **4Q PF operating margins reaching low-teens**. 3Q revenue guided **$3.45–3.60B (+158% y/y at the mid)** vs JPMe $3.366B / Street $3.445B. Backlog **$104.2B** (from $99.4B), **excluding $25B of net-new commitments added in early 3Q**.
- **⚠️ Mgmt's own market read, and it cuts both ways:** a **shift toward inferencing** and **shorter-duration contracts — but at better price points** (Barclays, 2026-08-12). Both effects are real; the page should stop treating backlog duration as monotonically good.

The bull PT ($150) did not move.**
- **⚠️ THE BEAR THAT DID NOT GO AWAY (Bernstein TMT desk / Tyler Seidman, 2026-08-12): "CRWV L/T headwinds persist though +N/T readx to AI supply chain."** Bernstein's framing keeps the long-term structural case (residual value, competitive intensity, customer concentration) separate from the near-term read-through, which it grants is positive for the AI supply chain. Same house flagged the read-through to **IREN** from the CRWV print plus the NVDA financing platform (Bernstein / Gautam Chhugani, 2026-08-12).
